# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-22

## LLM Agents
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Quant-Arena: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Quantitative Trading Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30113v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Quantitative research is an iterative loop: a researcher proposes a market hypothesis, implements it as an executable signal, tests it on historical data, and decides what to keep. Existing benchmarks score formulas, code, or individual trading decisions, leaving open whether large language models (LLMs) can complete this loop and produce strategies that work beyond the research period. We introduce Quant-Arena, a benchmark and public leaderboard for end-to-end quantitative research on real A-share market data. A model constructs features and stock-scoring code, receives feedback only on 2022--2023, and freezes one strategy for assessment on the sealed year 2024. A cash-account evaluator applies trading constraints and costs, including market impact estimated from a simulated order book. Scores are dominated by realized excess return and complemented by signal quality and the diversity of features used by the strategy. We evaluate nine frontier models, among which GPT-6 Astra scores highest; yet no model beats the equal-weight market in 2024. Quant-Arena is open to submissions from any model or agent harness.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://lab.einsia.ai/quant-arena</p></details> |
| **[From Text to Voice: A Reproducible and Verifiable Framework for Evaluating Tool Calling LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15104v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Voice agents increasingly require reliable tool use from speech, whereas prominent tool-calling benchmarks remain text-based. We study whether verified text benchmarks can be converted into controlled audio-based tool calling evaluations without re-annotating the tool schema and gold labels. Our dataset-agnostic framework uses text-to-speech, speaker variation, and environmental noise to create paired text-audio instances while preserving the original dataset annotations. Based on extensive evaluation of 7 omni-modal models on audio-converted versions of Confetti and When2Call, our framework demonstrates that the performance is strongly model- and task-dependent: Gemini-3.1-Flash-Live obtains the highest Confetti score (70.4), whereas GPT-Realtime-1.5 performs best on When2Call (71.9). On Confetti, the text-to-voice gap ranges from 1.8 points for Qwen3-Omni to 4.8 points for GPT-Realtime-1.5. A targeted analysis of failure cases demonstrates that degradations most often reflect misunderstandings of argument values in the speech. Considering real-world deployment scenarios, we further report text-only results, an ambiguity-based reformulation stress test, and a reference-free LLM-as-judge protocol validated against human preferences. Notably, we find that open-source Qwen3 judges with at least 8B parameters exceed 80% agreement with proprietary judges, supporting privacy-preserving evaluation. Overall, our framework provides a verifiable and reproducible first-stage diagnostic that complements purpose-built audio corpora.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Transactions on Machine Learning Research (TMLR). The final version will appear here: https://openreview.net/forum?id=69MeTLVKze</p></details> |
| **[Agent Behavior Maps: Locating Agents by What They Would Do, Without Running Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30014v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents with similar success rates can follow different action sequences. An Agent Behavior Map represents LLM-harness combinations using centered action log-likelihoods on shared reference trajectories, without requiring new environment rollouts from the target agent. We derive error bounds relating squared coordinate distances to trajectory KL under explicit assumptions, and across eight cross-scored models the distances track the directly measured trajectory KL in rank order. Across 100 LLMs and four environments, ALFWorld-based coordinates support performance prediction and identify behavioral similarities. In ALFWorld, these similarities persist after controlling for success rates and model size, including across model families. As an extension, harness-component displacements approximately compose, with accuracy varying across components and environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 5 figures, 12 tables. Under review</p></details> |
| **[ToM2Vec: Continuous Theory-of-Mind Tokens for Multi-Agent Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29934v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Forming an accurate theory-of-mind (ToM) is key to successful multi-agent collaboration and requires reasoning under uncertainty to capture multiple possible partner states. This is hard to do in the text representation space native to large language model (LLM) agents, which more easily describe a single partner state. To address this challenge, we introduce ToM2Vec, which learns to form abstract theories-of-mind in a rich latent space via a fixed budget of continuous reasoning tokens. During training, supervision for these tokens comes from the partner's actual thoughts, which the ToM tokens are trained to abstract and match. At test-time, ToM tokens are generated by each agent and act as conditioners for that agent's thoughts and actions. Across several environments covering collaborative, competitive, and mixed-motive settings (Overcooked-AI, multiparty negotiation, Decrypto, Hanabi, and Collab Escape/Capture), we evaluate ToM2Vec's ability to support multi-agent interactions and collaboration, finding consistent improvements over the base model and a variety of competitive trained and prompted baselines. We show that latent reasoning is critical, with performance dropping when reasoning is expressed in text. Moreover, we show that ToM2Vec training generalizes to partner model families not seen at train-time, including a proprietary model (GPT-5.4). In our analysis, we provide evidence that the latent tokens encode a ToM, rather than task-specific information: ToM2Vec's tokens improve the agent's ability to directly answer questions about partner beliefs, desires, and next actions.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/nsivaku/tom2vec</p></details> |
| **[FrontierCS 2: A Benchmark for Computer Science R&D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29932v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can LLM agents make contributions to frontier computer science R&D? We introduce FrontierCS 2, a benchmark of 48 open-ended tasks. We take a paper and the code its authors released and delete the paper's contribution. The deleted code becomes the human reference that sets the bar. Earlier open-ended benchmarks give each agent a problem statement and grade one program after the run. FrontierCS 2 places the agent inside a research environment. The agent inherits a working repository and may submit a patch at any point. For each patch, a judge returns a development-suite score but keeps the evaluator, the held-out final suite and the reference private, and grades the final submission on that suite against the reference. The tasks span 13 domains, from machine-learning systems and high-performance computing to databases, security and robotics. Beyond these 48 tasks, FrontierCS 2 keeps a private set of more than 100 tasks, to be announced in the future. We evaluate 12 agents. The best one is Claude Opus 5.5, passing 58% of its runs. No run passes on 16 tasks. A higher score is not the same as a pass: most runs that beat the reference on the combined final score still fall short on some metric or workload. On a shared capability scale for agents and tasks, the median task is roughly as difficult as what the strongest agent can handle.</p></details> | <details><summary>Websi...</summary><p>Website: https://frontier-cs.org ; Code: https://github.com/FrontierCS/FrontierCS-2</p></details> |
| **[RelAgent: LLM Agents as Data Scientists for Relational Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07840v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Relational learning is a challenging problem that has motivated a wide range of approaches, including graph-based models (e.g., graph neural networks, graph transformers), tabular methods (e.g., tabular foundation models), and sequence-based approaches (e.g., large language models), each with its own advantages and limitations. We propose RelAgent, an LLM-based autonomous data scientist for relational learning, which operates in two phases. In the search phase, an LLM agent uses database, validation, and evaluation workspace tools to construct SQL feature programs and select a predictive model. In the inference phase, the resulting program is executed without further LLM calls. The final predictor consists of SQL queries and a classical model, enabling fast, deterministic, and intrinsically interpretable predictions: features are human-readable queries, and predictions depend only on the resulting query-defined feature map, enabling scalable deployment using standard database systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Replayable Financial Agents: A Determinism-Faithfulness Assurance Harness for Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.15322v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Replaying a financial agent requires specifying the conditions of a rerun, preserving its execution record, and deciding which parts of its behavior to compare. We introduce the Determinism-Faithfulness Assurance Harness (DFAH), a framework and practical protocol for organizing these choices. The framework distinguishes comparisons of final decisions, tool-name sets, and ordered tool names, while treating support from available evidence as a separate evaluation target. We illustrate the framework using 2,957 recorded executions in synthetic compliance-triage and data-operations tasks. One data-operations configuration repeated its recorded decision across all trials on 84% of cases, while its ordered tool-name sequence repeated on 26%. Conversely, some compliance cases retained the same tool sequence while recorded decisions changed. A case-level example shows how repeated labels accompanied different lookups and escalation calls. These observations demonstrate how the comparison level changes the description of repeated behavior. The resulting protocol connects configuration control, preservation of each attempt, comparison of records, and inspection of changed cases. DFAH provides a practical foundation for designing replay experiments and locating execution differences during the development and evaluation of financial agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 2 figures, 5 tables. Replay framework and descriptive experiments in synthetic financial tasks. Includes an offline reproduction package. Original version accepted at the 2nd ICLR Workshop on Advances in Financial AI (2026)</p></details> |
| **[PersonalAI 2.0: Enhancing knowledge graph traversal/retrieval with planning mechanism for Personalized LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13481v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce PersonalAI 2.0 (PAI-2), a novel framework designed to enhance LLM-based systems through integration of external knowledge graphs (KGs). The proposed approach addresses key limitations of existing Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) methods by incorporating a dynamic, multistage pipeline. The central point of the PAI-2 design is its ability to perform adaptive, iterative information search, guided by extracted entities, matched graph vertices and generated clue-queries. Conducted evaluation on five benchmarks (Natural Questions, TriviaQA, HotpotQA, 2WikiMultihopQA and MuSiQue) demonstrates an improvement in the factual correctness of generated answers compared to baseline methods (LightRAG, RAPTOR, HippoRAG 2 and PAI-1). PAI-2 achieves a 9 percentage points (pp) average gain by LLM-as-a-Judge on 2WikiMultihopQA and MuSiQue benchmarks, and attains comparable accuracy to HippoRAG 2 on the TriviaQA and HotpotQA benchmarks with Qwen2.5 7B and multilingual-e5-large reflecting its effectiveness in reducing hallucination rates and increasing recall. We show that enabled search plan enhancement mechanism gain in average a 18 pp boost compared to disabled one by LLM-as-a-Judge across five benchmarks. In addition, an ablation study reveals that PAI-2 achieves superior result on the MINE-1 benchmark, obtaining an 89\% information-retention score with Qwen2.5 7B and multilingual-e5-large. Collectively, these findings underscore the potential of PAI-2 to serve as a reusable component for personalized AI applications, which require context-aware knowledge-representation and reasoning capabilities. The source code of PAI-2 is available at the following link: https://github.com/Dzigen/PersonalAI.</p></details> |  |
| **[Effects of Theory of Mind and Prosocial Beliefs on Steering Human-Aligned Behaviors of LLMs in Ultimatum Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24255v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown potential in simulating human behaviors and performing theory-of-mind (ToM) reasoning, crucial for complex social interactions. We investigate ToM reasoning's role in aligning agentic behaviors with human norms in negotiation tasks, using the ultimatum game as our referenced task. We initialized LLM agents with different prosocial beliefs (Greedy, Fair, Selfless) and reasoning methods (chain of thought and ToM reasoning of varying levels), examining their decision-making processes and outcomes across multiple LLMs, including reasoning models like o3-mini and DeepSeek-R1 Distilled Qwen 32B. We perform 2,700 simulations to show that ToM reasoning enhances behavioral alignment with human norms, decision-making consistency, and negotiation outcomes. Consistent with prior findings, reasoning LLMs exhibit limited capability compared to ToM-enhanced LLMs, with different game roles benefiting from different ToM orders. Agents almost always chose strategies consistent with their assigned beliefs, and doing so aligned better with human norms in nearly every role, yet Greedy and Selfless agents still fell short of their norms in outcomes. Human verification further revealed that Llama 3.3 70B produces reasoning most consistent with its actions and beliefs. Our findings advance understanding of ToM's role in human-AI interaction and cooperative decision-making. The code used for our experiments can be found at https://github.com/Stealth-py/UltimatumToM.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to AACL-IJCNLP 2026. v2 corrects the RQ4 analysis (Section 5.4, Fig. 4); other results remain unchanged</p></details> |
| **[TabAgent: Distilling LLM Agent Components into Cheap Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16429v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic systems, AI architectures that autonomously execute multi-step workflows to achieve complex goals, are often built using repeated large language model (LLM) calls for closed-set decision tasks such as routing, shortlisting, gating, and verification. While convenient, this design makes deployments slow and expensive due to cumulative latency and token usage. We propose TabAgent, a framework for replacing generative decision components in closed-set selection tasks with a compact textual-tabular classifier trained on execution traces. TabAgent (i) extracts structured schema, state, and dependency features from trajectories (TabSchema), (ii) augments coverage with schema-aligned synthetic supervision (TabSynth), and (iii) scores candidates with a lightweight classifier (TabHead). On the long-horizon AppWorld benchmark, TabAgent maintains task-level success while eliminating shortlist-time LLM calls, reducing latency by approximately 95% and inference cost by 85-91%. Beyond tool shortlisting, TabAgent generalizes to other agentic decision heads, establishing a paradigm for learned discriminative replacements of generative bottlenecks in production agent architectures.</p></details> | <details><summary>67 pa...</summary><p>67 pages, 14 figures, 26 tables. v2 is a substantial revision with a new title and two added authors. The evaluation is extended from AppWorld to seven agent benchmarks across three decision families (set retrieval, classification, policy and reward), and TabHead is now a CatBoost classifier with an ablation over classical and neural heads</p></details> |
| **[DynaWeave: Learning to Adapt through Dynamic Scenario Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29443v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use tools to complete tasks in stateful environments. However, many training environments remain largely static apart from changes caused by the agent's own actions, whereas external events introduced by other users, services, or sudden contingencies in real environments can invalidate ongoing plans. To address this gap, we introduce DynaWeave, an automated synthesis framework that transforms existing static tasks into verifiable dynamic scenarios. The framework generates exogenous events relevant to each task, covering changes in task requirements, environment state, and external information. It uses directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) to represent dependencies between events and an execution engine to activate them during agent interaction. A dynamic verifier revises only the evaluation checks affected by events that actually occur and retains all other checks. Using DynaWeave, we collect 2,095 verified interaction trajectories across 36 environments and use them for supervised fine-tuning of Qwen3.5 models. Experiments on Gaia2, CostBench, and ToolMaze show that DynaWeave achieves consistent improvements in overall performance across all three evaluated model sizes, increasing the task completion rate on CostBench by 67.34 percentage points and even exceeding the performance of Gemini-3.1-Pro-Preview on ToolMaze. These results support dynamic scenario synthesis as a practical approach to training agents to adapt to changing environments. Our code is available at https://github.com/Miaow-Lab/DynaWeave.</p></details> |  |
| **[One Act, Many Masks: Effect-Marginal Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29427v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in multi-turn reinforcement learning (RL) have substantially improved the performance of LLM agents on complex, long-horizon interactive tasks. However, despite progress in fine-grained credit assignment, policy optimization still applies these learning signals through response-specific probability ratios. Different textual responses can induce the same executable effect, yet credit differences arising from subsequent outcomes remain tied to their individual textual realizations. We identify this mismatch as credit-update misalignment. Our analysis reveals that credit-text pairing directly shapes policy-update directions, even when the policy context and executable effect are held fixed. Motivated by this finding, we propose Effect-Marginal Policy Optimization (EMPO), which aligns policy optimization with execution-level equivalence through an effect-level surrogate that couples updates across alternative textual realizations. EMPO preserves the original credit signals, integrates with both trajectory-level and step-level credit estimators, and requires no additional critics or rollouts. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Sokoban show that EMPO outperforms strong baselines in both task performance and learning efficiency.</p></details> | 20 pages, 11 figures |
| **[Benchmarking and Error Analysis of Deep Search and Deep Research LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29346v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As Large Language Models (LLMs) evolve into autonomous Deep Search and Deep Research Agents capable of executing long-horizon, multi-step tasks, evaluating their reliability becomes increasingly critical. However, existing evaluation paradigms predominantly focus on final task outcomes, leaving a significant gap in understanding the intermediate execution traces, where agents are highly susceptible to various errors. To address this critical gap, we introduce a comprehensive methodology and analytical framework designed specifically to diagnose errors in intermediate workflows. Building upon this framework, we establish a structured taxonomy that categorizes these vulnerabilities into two primary dimensions: System-Level Errors and Task-Level Errors. Through a systematic examination of step-by-step execution traces and agent-environment interactions, we reveal how these underlying errors affect overall performance. Finally, through empirical experiments and systematic analysis of the identified error types, we provide actionable design guidelines, aiming to enhance the robustness, reasoning capabilities, and reliability of Deep Search and Deep Research agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proce...</summary><p>Proceedings of the 1st ICML Workshop on Failure Modes in Agentic AI (FAGEN), Seoul, South Korea, 2026</p></details> |
| **[From Prompts to Posteriors: Can LLM Agents Perform Bayesian Inference in Context?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29331v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied across the scientific discovery pipeline, from synthesizing literature to generating hypotheses to informing experimental design. A unifying principle behind these tasks is the need to continuously update beliefs as evidence accumulates, for which Bayesian inference provides a natural framework. However, existing work assumes that LLMs cannot carry out principled Bayesian belief updating in context, instead relying on simplifications and heuristics. To challenge this assumption, we present a principled evaluation of Bayesian reasoning capabilities in LLMs, analyzing both the model's internal consistency as well as its ability to recover an externally determined posterior. Specifically, we build an evaluation framework spanning qualitative epistemological examples to probe probabilistic coherence, quantitative benchmarks of Bayesian inference capabilities, and analyses of the impact of non-Bayesian updates on downstream task performance. Across different scenarios, we find that LLMs generally violate Bayes' rule when updating their beliefs in context. However, when equipped with modern reasoning abilities and code execution capabilities, their performance drastically improves, enabling even the close approximation of analytically intractable posteriors. Our evaluation shows that current LLMs with access to appropriate tools could already be used to make complex belief updates in scientific discovery, enabling rapid acceleration of the efficiency and scale of AI-powered scientific discovery.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 6 figures in main text</p></details> |
| **[AgentMonopoly: Behavioral Patterns and Persona Control in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29330v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How do LLM agents' strategies differ, and how much can instructions to adopt a persona change their behavior over a game? We study these questions with AgentMonopoly, an environment where agents communicate, buy and trade properties, and make decisions that affect later turns. We compare models from four families, each at two sizes, in games with identical models, different sizes of the same family, or different families. We also assign one agent a persona, a description of how it should behave, intended to change a tendency observed in games without that persona. The tested models differ in how often they trade, waive rent, and manage cash and properties. Larger models do not consistently win more games. Persona instructions change the words agents use and some of their actions, but the changes do not always match the role across all decisions. These results show why persona following should be assessed through actions and outcomes as well as language. As our findings are conditional on the tested checkpoints, prompts, and five seeds per configuration, the open-source nature of AgentMonopoly provides the experimental infrastructure for further extending these comparisons. Our code is available at https://github.com/SuELS-Group/Agent_Monopoly.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 8 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[Seeing the Forest for the Trees: Emergent Agreement from Individual Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29328v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM agents interact in the wild and shape each other's decisions, understanding how they converge toward agreement becomes important. We study this in multi-turn conversations where two LLMs discuss subjective matters, can agree or disagree, and may change their stances. Using a linear classifier on individual conversation activations, we identify an internal representation of agreement. We find that it varies substantially across turns and conversations and does not reliably track external agreement at the individual conversation level. We therefore move beyond the representation of agreement in individual conversations, by aggregating the agreement representation across conversations. Externally, this aggregate view reveals a simple structure: when pooled across conversations, agreement can be predicted well with a Markov process, with agreement at each turn being a direct function of agreement at the previous turn. Similarly, when internal agreement is aggregated, it couples with the external Markov agreement through a power-law mapping, exposing Markovian dynamics in internal representations. Additionally, we find that after a change of stance, models express agreement externally before their internal representations reflect the same shift. These results reveal a joint structure between internal and external agreement that emerges only when internal features are aggregated across conversations. Code available at https://github.com/technion-cs-nlp/SeeingTheForestForTheTrees.</p></details> |  |
| **[Signals and Queries: Token-Efficient Agentic Context Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29326v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>User-facing LLM agents must identify which behavioral policies apply at each turn, but evaluating every policy with an LLM is costly. We introduce Signals and Queries (S&Q), a lightweight first-stage relevance filter developed for Parlant 4.0 that removes at least 30% of candidate policies before downstream evaluation while preserving high recall. For each policy, an LLM generates activating examples and hard negatives offline. An independent logistic classifier trained on their embeddings then predicts relevance using embeddings and vector dot products, with no per-turn LLM calls. This shifts expensive relevance evaluation to a smaller candidate set, while allowing each policy's classifier to be updated independently. Evaluated against 9,144 LLM-judge relevance labels covering 254 $τ^2$-bench conversation prefixes and 36 airline policies, S&Q achieves higher precision than top-K and cosine-threshold retrieval at every evaluated recall level, with consistent results across two embedding providers. These findings support inexpensive, per-policy classification as a practical first stage for reducing downstream evaluation workload in customer-facing agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[Act on Your Behalf, Beyond Your Authorization: Benchmarking LLM Agent Authorization Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27548v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When an LLM agent acts on behalf of a user, it is expected to act within the authorization scope the user grants. Existing agent evaluations largely measure task completion or harmful consequences and can therefore overlook actions that violate this delegation without producing conventional safety or privacy harms. We introduce AuthorizationBench, a benchmark of authorization alignment grounded in documented real-world cases. A scalable construction pipeline produces 286 executable tasks spanning situational triggers, the presence or absence of direct harmful consequences, and ten application domains. We execute every task with four models at two reasoning-effort settings across three agent harnesses in an authorization-aware sandbox that keeps authorized and out-of-authorization paths executable and observable, yielding 6,864 trajectories. Agents act out of authorization in 19.2% of trajectories, with rates ranging from 10.5% to 26.6% across the 24 configurations. Task completion does not imply authorization. Increasing reasoning effort leaves the aggregate rate nearly unchanged, with effects varying across models. On controlled pairs, agents act out of authorization at similar rates with (17.9%) and without (20.8%) direct harmful consequences, and more often under benign pressures than malicious injections (23.5% versus 17.6%, p=0.002). Our results show that task capability, additional reasoning, and consequence-oriented safety evaluation do not by themselves ensure authorization alignment. Moreover, agents that consult the principal before completing a task exhibit lower rates of out-of-authorization behavior (14.0% versus 21.6%, p<0.001), pointing to a tension between agent autonomy and preserving users' decision authority that calls for further human-centered study. Dataset and code are available at https://github.com/PEACH-Research-Lab/AuthorizationBench .</p></details> |  |
| **[CrashGym: Measuring the Decision Value of Experimentation for LLM Agents in Counterfactual Market Crashes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29245v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language-model agents are increasingly asked to run experiments, yet benchmarks rarely measure whether those experiments improve the decisions they are meant to inform. We introduce CrashGym, a deterministic, replayable limit-order-book simulator in which an agent must choose between a deleveraging intervention and a liquidity backstop for a market about to suffer a shock-triggered crash. Before committing, the agent may query intervention experiments in a laboratory realization of the market. Its choice is then scored on an independently seeded target realization in which the simulator executes every feasible intervention, so regret relative to the best intervention is computed by directly simulating every alternative on the same target repeats, with no human or model-based judging. The simulator implements each amplification channel---persistent price impact, liquidity withdrawal, margin-driven liquidation, and an evolving strategy mix---as an explicit mechanism that can be switched on in stages. In a prospectively specified study on 24 newly sampled worlds, laboratory access reduces the mean regret of the one deployment with a complete record (Luna) from 8.878 to 3.785, a paired reduction of 5.092 utility units (nominal 95% world-bootstrap interval: 1.028--9.507), or 57.4% of the OBSERVE-ONLY mean. Prospective comparisons for two further deployments are incomplete; in a separate exploratory study, mean regret is lower with laboratory access for all four deployments evaluated. A laboratory whose strategy mix evolves endogenously yields no established advantage over one with a fixed mix. CrashGym thus measures the value of experimentation as avoided decision loss and lets simulator detail be judged by the decisions it supports.</p></details> | 14 pages |
| **[When Agent Evaluation Becomes Unreliable: An Empirical Study of Oracle Failures and Configuration-Dependent Measurement in an LLM Agent Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16067v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent evaluation harnesses are software: they run a test suite, drive a test input generator, and decide outcomes with a test oracle. We audit them for defects a score table cannot show. First, a systematic oracle-integrity auditing approach - five state-integrity invariants and three known-answer controls - is measured by injecting seven oracle defect classes across two task domains and a synthetic domain. Every class is detected, and on the 57 separable variant-target combinations it detects 54; the repaired oracle yields no false positive. On a widely used agent benchmark, it exposes a reward function that resets the database to build a ground-truth hash and never restores it, so a harness scoring afterwards reads the reference solution's state: recorded labels report 4,194 database passes over 4,320 episodes, replay gives 2,883, and success falls from 94.3% to 65.5%. The correction is not a uniform offset; it removes 24 to 34 points from each agent and changes which agent this matrix measures as strongest. Second, more weakly, localising the apparatus is a property of the measurement: a 3,240-episode ablation associates the three documents with lower collapse in the tested configuration, without ordering them, and a second task domain reverses the language effect. Third, the method did not transfer to a second, independently implemented benchmark; we name the contract required. A replication package recomputes every analysis offline with no model calls.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to Empirical Software Engineering. v2: Expanded related work, clarified methodological and statistical limitations, and added replay-corrected agent comparisons and supporting replication materials</p></details> |
| **[5uGUE: Agentic Hardware-in-the-Loop Fuzzing for 5G Devices and beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29155v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we present 5uGUE, a large language model (LLM)-driven hardware-in-the-loop agent, which enables the wireless fuzzing of 5G-enabled devices (such as mobile phones). 5uGUE combines multi-step reasoning with tool-assisted interaction and integrates into the fuzzing pipeline by leveraging specialized tools for internet search, 5G protocol analysis, memory management, and test-case execution. The agent iteratively generates test cases, executes them on targets, collects runtime feedback, and adaptively refines the fuzzing strategy. We evaluate 5uGUE on five commercial off-the-shelf (COTS) 5G devices across six LLMs. The best performing runs for 5uGUE trigger 167 crashes in total (141 novel and 31 unique) within 60 interaction turns (49 to 133 minutes total runtime). In contrast, the best runs for the state-of-the-art baseline (5GHOUL) discovers only 13 crashes in total (10 novel and 6 unique) within 150 minutes. Overall, by integrating LLM-driven agents, 5uGUE demonstrates significantly better crash discovery performance (12.8x total, 5.2x unique improvement). Notably, among the crashes discovered by 5uGUE, MediaTek has confirmed two new high-severity vulnerabilities, with one CVE assigned (CVE-2026-20525), while UNISOC confirmed seven crashes. Finally, to demonstrate that 5uGUE is agnostic to wireless protocols, we also extend it to Bluetooth Classic with only minimal code changes. In our evaluations, 5uGUE finds an average of 6.4 novel crashes and 1.6 unique crashes. In a nutshell, 5uGUE highlights that LLM agents are capable to operate effectively beyond software-in-the-loop settings, extending autonomous vulnerability discovery to the proprietary, hardware-bound systems that affect billions of end-user devices across multiple wireless protocols.</p></details> |  |

A merge is admitted only when the child moves toward the donor, past the child the same operator produces at zero dose, and the lower confidence bound on that movement reaches half the requested dose. That is the admission test. A detectable shortfall rejects the operator, and the test is otherwise undetermined. We keep realised fitness φas the selector and reference performance θas the unused ruler. Every measurement uses gemini-3.1-flash-lite on one negotiation task, and no fixed-oracle arm was run. In the open loop, tactic append grafts short donor rules and is admitted. Free rewrite moves only a third of the requested dose and is rejected. Template append pastes raw donor replies into the child. Its movement clears the bound on the first probe but not on the re-probe, and it is left undetermined. A held-out re-probe on the five situations the merge never saw confirms that both appends move, and it does not rescue free rewrite. Neither append rises with the dose. Constrained rewrite is admitted in the open loop and was not run in the closed loop, which changes only the operator. The population, the scores and the task stay fixed. No free-rewrite run beats the best generation-zero member. Every tactic-append run and every template-append run does. Sampled loop children keep that split. Free rewrite moves no further toward the donor, and both appends still clear the bound, by less than in the open loop.</p></details> |  |

With AKV, it achieves up to 1.2x the output throughput and a 1.5x speedup in P95 request latency. Recursive self-improvement of cache policies further reduces cost by 7.1% over expert design. Our code is at https://github.com/thu-nics/AKV.</p></details> |  |
| **[OptScientist: Automated Discovery of Optimization Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28562v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many scientific phenomena are best understood as outcomes of underlying optimization problems, yet automated scientific discovery has largely focused on recovering equations that describe observed input-output patterns. We introduce OptScientist, a novel automated framework for discovering compact symbolic optimization laws directly from data. OptScientist comprises (i) a model that infers symbolic optimization problems by first learning to represent data as an optimization problem and then extracting its symbolic form, and (ii) an LLM agentic system embedding the model in a discovery process that integrates theoretical priors from scientific literature, discovers candidate laws, abstracts their underlying mechanisms, and suggests new data to collect. We first evaluate OptScientist on OptFeynman, a novel benchmark of 99 physics-derived optimization problems, where OptScientist recovers ground-truth laws for over 80% of the problems and remains robust to noisy contextual information, while substantially outperforming machine-learning, symbolic, and LLM-based model-discovery baselines. On human risky choice and central-bank monetary policy, OptScientist discovers compact symbolic optimization laws that achieve held-out predictive performance competitive with leading baselines, while using few parameters and substantially fewer tokens than LLM baselines. Overall, OptScientist extends automated scientific discovery to uncovering optimization laws that generate observed phenomena, enabling automated theory building across the physical, biological, behavioral, and social sciences.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project website: https://optscientist.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[Foundational Refinement Proofs for EVM Bytecode, at the Price of Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26306v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Relating executable code to a high-level model of its behavior has been a central concern of programming-language research for decades. Formally verified compilers, translation validators and certifying compilers address this problem through different trade-offs between proof effort, automation, generality, and the size of the trusted base. Agentic proof development is now capable of producing machine-checked proofs, sometimes unsupervised, at a scale and speed that were previously out of reach. We propose a verification architecture that takes advantage of this shift. An executable obtains a machine-checked proof that it behaves according to a high-level model, independently of the compiler that produced it. The proof is written by an LLM agent. We evaluate the feasibility of this architecture for the Ethereum Virtual Machine (EVM). We build EquiVM, a foundational framework in Lean comprising an executable EVM semantics and a formal specification language, Stele, that gives a high-level authoritative model of the bytecode's behavior, but commits to no source language or compilation toolchain. We state what it means for EVM bytecode to refine a Stele spec and we build a proof library to facilitate proof construction. We evaluate the capabilities of frontier LLMs to construct such proofs, as well as the cost of proof production and repair. Our results show that agents prove 35 real production contracts end to end, without modifying their source or bytecode, among them core contracts of Safe, Morpho, Compound and Uniswap protocols, with bytecode of size close to the maximum the EVM allows. Several proofs need only the initial prompt, the rest a handful of human inputs, and existing proofs are repaired in a fraction of the original time when the source or the compiler configuration changes.</p></details> |  |
| **[After the Attacker Leaves: Benchmarking Carryover Attacks on LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28425v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern LLM agents persist state across sessions via two channels: long-lived memory files silently prepended to the system prompt, and scheduled tasks waking the agent without user input. Existing prompt-injection benchmarks evaluate attacks only in the session where malicious content is first observed, leaving the gate governing when and how often a planted payload later re-fires unmeasured. We present CarryoverBench: 12 personal-assistant scenarios and 101 attack tasks across three trigger channels: Instant, same-turn IPI; Autonomous, heartbeat-scheduled; and Latent, memory-resurfaced. A rule-based verifier scores planting and triggering as independent stages. Across five frontier models, planted payloads re-fire without measurable decay over five rounds; conditional on successful planting, the trigger stage often provides little additional protection. | **[Self-Credit: Credit Assignment for Long-Horizon Agentic RL via Intra-group Rescaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28388v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are commonly trained with RLVR, but the sparse nature of typical RLVR rewards makes credit assignment challenging, especially for long-horizon tasks. First, during its rollouts, the policy model generates summaries with citations to earlier turns containing critical evidence, which serve as self-generated credit. Second, we refine this model-generated credit with intra-group rescaling, which compares cited actions across successful and failed rollouts for the same prompt. This approach uses group-level agreement and disagreement to remove undeserved credit and further emphasize credit assigned to critical actions. Third, advantage reweighting uses the rescaled credit weights to update the advantages of the cited turns. Analyses show that self-generated credit provides useful signals for selecting critical actions, and that intra-group rescaling, especially on failed rollouts, is important across model scales.</p></details> |  |
| **[FALCA: Falsification-Aware Reinforcement Learning with Hierarchical Credit Assignment for Long-Horizon Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28361v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon deep search requires LLM agents to iteratively decompose complex information needs, gather evidence from external sources, and maintain a reliable research state under multiple constraints. Project page: https://douzj-llm.github.io/FALCA/</p></details> |
| **[Disclosure Begets Disclosure: A Self-Reinforcing Privacy Loop in LLM Agents with Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28315v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use persistent memory to personalize responses, and personal attributes in this memory pose a privacy risk if exposed. We ask how consulting memory during dialogue changes what users go on to say, and thus what the memory comes to reveal. Across four support models, memory raises response quality but also empirical leakage, the share of attribute values correctly inferred from the record, by a margin that widens over sessions. The cause is a loop in ordinary, non-adversarial assistance. Memory-conditioned responses carry earlier values forward, often as presuppositions, and users affirm them or leave them uncorrected, so that cues and the agent's own guesses become recorded values. The loop reinforces itself, and removing the agent's mentions of an attribute lowers its later disclosure about twice as much as single-turn effects predict. Users cannot leave it on their own, since correcting a value discloses the attribute, declining to discuss it shifts later predictions toward sensitive values, and incorrect values left in place become entrenched. The loop is best weakened at the agent's response. Instructing the agent not to state or presuppose attributes brings leakage to about the no-memory level while keeping most of the quality gains, whereas filtering or anonymizing what users say or what is stored gives up much of them.</p></details> |  |
| **[Local Work, Global Progress: A Convergence Theory of AI Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28286v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Teams of large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly applied to complex tasks, but their collaboration protocols are mostly designed empirically, without convergence guarantees. We formalize collaborative problem solving as block optimization: each agent improves one part of a shared solution, guided by its local objective plus a correction for how its changes affect the other parts. A coordinator checks every proposal against the global objective and merges proposals that do not interfere with each other. We prove linear convergence up to an error floor that vanishes when the corrections are exact. Interfering proposals can also be merged once their combination has been checked; the rate then depends on how well the proposals actually combine, not on worst-case interactions. With GPT-OSS-120B agents on robot routing, a three-agent team finds collision-free plans in every run, better than those of a single agent that controls all robots. On a larger routing task, a six-agent team completes its search about twice as fast as the single agent (in median). Merging the best checked combination of proposals, rather than only proposals that do not interfere, cuts the team's search time by about 30%. With constants measured on the runs, our convergence bound holds in every round and follows the observed progress. We also test the theory on query planning in SQLite and on a synthetic task.</p></details> | <details><summary>59 pa...</summary><p>59 pages, 15 figures, 28 tables. Code: https://github.com/vectozavr/local-work-global-progress</p></details> |
| **[MaD-RSI: Model-as-Data for Recursive Self-Improvement of Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28201v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recursive self-improvement (RSI) of agent harnesses has emerged as an effective approach to enhancing the capabilities of large language model (LLM) agents. However, such optimization often creates a strong coupling between the learned harness and its target LLM, limiting generalization across models and requiring substantial computational resources to adapt to new LLMs. Moreover, existing RSI methods typically optimize a harness using feedback from a single model and evaluate its generalization to other models only after training. This paradigm treats model differences primarily as a criterion for evaluating generalization rather than as valuable evidence for optimization. In this paper, we introduce a new perspective that treats models as data and places the harness at the center of optimization. Building on this perspective, we propose \textsc{MaD-RSI}, a framework that directly optimizes a shared harness across multiple heterogeneous LLMs by incorporating their diverse failure trajectories into the training process. Rather than assessing model differences only after optimization, \textsc{MaD-RSI} leverages these differences as training evidence to guide the self-improvement of a shared harness. Furthermore, we introduce a new evaluation framework with two complementary metrics designed to assess the effectiveness of harness optimization across models. Experiments across multiple datasets and LLMs demonstrate that \textsc{MaD-RSI} improves generalization over existing approaches while reducing training costs.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgenTest: Mining Business Rules to Test LLM Agents for Business Logic Bugs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28116v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly execute business operations through tools and interact directly with application data and services. Beyond completing user requests, these agents must obey domain-specific business rules that constrain when actions are permitted and what data or resources may be affected. Violating such rules can lead to business logic bugs, even when a task appears successful. Detecting these bugs is difficult because the rules are rarely available as explicit, testable specifications. They are distributed across system prompts and tool definitions, while others remain implicit in domain knowledge. Existing agent testing approaches typically evaluate task success, predefined policies, or known attack scenarios. This limits their ability to systematically uncover previously unspecified business-rule violations. We present AgenTest, which mines business rules from system prompts, tool definitions, and domain knowledge, refines them into Given-When-Then specifications, and systematically tests each specification. For each extracted specification, AgenTest instantiates its Given conditions by constructing a satisfying environment state and simulated user, exercises the agent through multi-turn interactions, and checks its Then conditions over messages, tool calls, and state changes using a hybrid oracle. On the airline, retail, and telecom agents of tau2-bench with two underlying models, AgenTest produces 551 refined specifications, and detects violations of 89 and 121 distinct specifications, respectively -- 3.4X and 2.5X as many as all baselines combined. Moreover, about 60% of bug-triggering runs cannot be detected from the final state alone, highlighting the need to check the full interaction against explicit business rules.</p></details> |  |
| **[ClawMMU: Protection-Coherent Virtual Memory for Stateful LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28093v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents keep long-term memory, much of which comes from web pages and tool results. ClawMMU checks these labels whenever content enters the model's context, is written to storage, returns from storage, or drives a tool call. Because the checks depend on where content came from, not on what it says, memory attacks become classical protection faults that the harness stops even when the model is fooled.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the AgenticOS Workshop @ NeurIPS 2026 (non-archival). 6 pages, 1 figure, 1 table</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking How to Remember: Beyond Atomic Facts in Lifelong LLM Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19952v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To enable reliable long-term interaction, LLM agents require a memory system that can faithfully store, efficiently retrieve, and deeply reason over accumulated dialogue history. Nevertheless, such fact-centric designs inevitably discard fine-grained details in original dialogues and fail to support deep reasoning over scattered isolated facts. To address these limitations, we propose TriMem, which maintains three coexisting representation granularities, including raw dialogue segments anchored by source identifiers for storage fidelity, extracted atomic facts for efficient memory retrieval, synthesized profiles that aggregate dispersed facts into holistic semantic understanding for deep reasoning. We further adopt TextGrad-based prompt optimization, which iteratively refines extraction and profiling prompts via response quality feedback, achieving lifelong evolution without any parameter updating. Extensive experiments on LoCoMo and PerLTQA across multiple LLM backbones demonstrate that TriMem consistently outperforms strong memory baselines. The code is available at https://TMLR-TriMem.github.io .</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[QwenGyre: An Elastic Reinforcement Learning Framework for Training xLong-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33848v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly undertake extreme-long (xlong) horizon tasks, where a single execution can span hours, hundreds of model--environment interactions, and nearly 1M tokens per rollout. QwenGyre elastically reallocates GPUs between rollout and training without interrupting live executions, while its trajectory processor reconstructs branching histories, scores partial progress, and deduplicates redundant paths to bound training costs. | **[SAPD: Learning from Agent Experience without Environment Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27847v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scaling the training of Large Language Model (LLM) agents typically requires a large collection of executable environments, whose construction, maintenance, and operation constrain the expansion of training experience. To address this challenge, we propose Step-Advantage Policy Distillation (SAPD), a method for learning from offline trajectories using relative step advantages. SAPD decomposes multi-turn trajectories into individual decision contexts for on-policy distillation, allowing the student to generate responses conditioned on recorded histories, and introduces relative step advantage signals to stabilize training. | **[Toward Agentic AI for the 6G Physical Layer: From Algorithm Discovery to Verified Baseband Silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27797v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>6G will be designed faster than it can be built: AI-driven search already proposes physical-layer algorithms at machine speed, while carrying a single one onto embedded baseband silicon remains specialist work. That silicon imposes hard memory and numeric limits, but also exposes cost structures a GPU lacks, and a design flow that never runs on the target sees neither. We present an agentic workflow in which an LLM coding agent implements algorithms on a commercial baseband SoC, optimizes them down to agent-written vector assembly, and validates them on silicon. The agent is guided by a knowledge base of measured platform facts, checked at every step against evaluators it is instructed never to edit, and metered per phase. We showcase the workflow on an AI-discovered orthogonal time frequency space (OTFS) detector portfolio on the open 6G4All platform. It yields twelve kernels that run cycle-exactly on silicon, their optimized versions at 18k-405k cycles (30-660 $μ$s) per frame. It also yields a new detector shaped by the chip's measured costs that needs 13.9% fewer cycles than the port of the discovered detector while matching its error rate, with a measured edge our evaluation cannot statistically resolve, a structure that a hardware-blind search objective had no reason to find. What is not specific to OTFS is the workflow: an LLM agent exploring an implementation space under real hardware limits, with the cost of every choice measured rather than assumed.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages, 3 figures, 5 tables. Accepted at the 2026 IEEE Globecom Workshops (GC Wkshps), 2nd Workshop on Agentic AI Empowered Wireless Communications and Networking, Macau, China, December 2026. (c) 2026 IEEE</p></details> |
| **[GRADUS: Discovering and Recovering Agent Lessons from the Environment's Latent Syllabus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27781v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training LLM agents for policy-constrained tool environments, such as an airline backend with booking, refund, and cancellation policies, requires tasks that exercise the environment's rules individually and in combination. Crucially, Gradus jointly calibrates tasks and solutions against the environment: execution feedback identifies which side to refine while preserving the intended behavioral requirements. | **[RITE: Evaluating End-to-End Resilience to Silent Tool Errors in E-Commerce Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27777v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using LLM agents increasingly answer e-commerce queries through multi-step tool interactions. | **[SAFE-Mem: Secure Agentic Memory with Fine-Grained Authority and Governance-Aware Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22959v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on persistent memory to support long-term reasoning and personalized interactions. First, SAFE-Mem annotates each claim with three governance labels (i.e., kind, authority, and scope) to capture its provenance and permitted use. Second, governance-aware retrieval matches query requirements with claim permissions to block unauthorized influence while retaining legitimate information. | **[Is AI Widening the Wage Gap? A Hybrid Agentic Simulation for Labor Equity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33367v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Artificial intelligence (AI) is reshaping labor markets, yet its effects on wage distribution and the underlying mechanisms remain insufficiently understood. Using this framework and sociodemographic data from China, we simulate changes in wage distribution under repeated AI shocks. The results show that both the average-wage ratio between workers in the top and bottom income deciles (T10/B10) and the Gini coefficient increase persistently, suggesting that AI shocks widen the wage gap and exacerbate income inequality. This pattern of a widening wage gap remains robust across alternative large language model decision engines and 30 Monte Carlo simulations. We further conduct counterfactual policy experiments. The results show that education subsidies targeted at low-income workers increase both the number of skill-upgrading attempts and the number of successful upgrades, with particularly pronounced improvements in the upskilling success probability of workers in the bottom income decile. | **[Local Allow, Global Deny: Why Per-Action Authorization Does Not Compose in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27620v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous LLM agents act through tools under per-action authorization: a local guard screens each tool call in isolation. We show that local authorization does not compose into global authorization. A compositional authorization failure (CAF) is a locally authorized execution, or a pair of locally authorized executions, that violates a global policy spanning intent, purpose, provenance, consent, and delegation. We characterize CAFs along two axes, expressiveness (single-trace safety versus relational k-safety) and observability (a ladder of monitor profiles). Our central result is an impossibility: relational purpose violations form a 2-safety hyperproperty with no per-trace locus of blame, so a per-action monitor that never raises a false alarm detects none of them, independent of the underlying model or decision procedure; they become visible only under cross-execution monitoring, which none of the six guardrails we survey implements. We make this constructive with AL-SYNTH, a SAT/SMT synthesizer (using no language model) that emits minimal CAF witnesses, each checked against an exhaustive oracle. The empirical signature is sharp: across nine LLM judges the per-action view approves the synthesized witness almost without exception, blocking it at a rate comparable to a benign control, yet the same models flag it when shown the two executions together. Per-action authorization is therefore structurally insufficient for relational policy conjuncts not reducible to single-trace safety: to bound them, a monitor must compare executions or accept false alarms.</p></details> | <details><summary>Exten...</summary><p>Extended version of a paper accepted at the 8th IEEE International Conference on Trust, Privacy and Security in Intelligent Systems and Applications (IEEE TPS 2026), with full proofs and appendices. 12 pages, 5 figures, 7 tables. Code and data: https://github.com/anmolpandey299/al-synth</p></details> |
| **[Auditing the Product, Not the Recursion: The Evaluation Evidence Behind Recursive Self-Improvement Claims](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27610v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recursive self-improvement (RSI) promises AI systems, such as self-evolving LLM agents, that become better at producing further improvements. Evaluating this promise requires separating product claims about the final system from process claims about the improvement process itself. We examine this question through full-text coding of 114 eligible studies in a reproducible arXiv search frame frozen in October 2026, including 90 that describe their own systems as RSI. The codebook links claims to evaluation designs. Independent human coding, third-reader adjudication, and a separate blind coding of improver comparisons assess coding reliability. Among the 90 RSI studies, 90% report product evaluation beyond development tasks under a broad criterion that includes partial or unstated separation, while 60% report evaluation on tasks unused for selection. Only 22% audit two or more reruns of the full loop. In a human-coded random sample, the RSI studies show the same contrast: 45% evaluate on tasks unused for selection and 14% audit two or more reruns. Of the 26 studies claiming a better improvement procedure, 23 report a related comparison, but only 4 compare earlier and later procedures from identical starting artifacts at a matched budget. Across these results and a comparison set of established works, evidence for repeatability and improved procedures remains less developed than product evaluation. Three propositions explain how selection effects, shared evaluator errors, and changes in system state limit what product evaluations can establish. Together, the findings support a claim-indexed reporting standard that connects each claim to the evaluation design needed to test it.</p></details> | <details><summary>39 pa...</summary><p>39 pages, 1 figure, 20 tables</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Experience: Knowledge-Guided Evolution for NPU Kernel Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27588v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents have become capable kernel engineers on mainstream GPU stacks, but they struggle on emerging accelerators such as Ascend NPUs, whose programming model is barely represented in pretraining data. We argue that experience alone is not enough for kernel generation. An agent can only remember what it has tried, and it rarely tries a mechanism it does not know. As a result, such memory offers little help when a new operator calls for a different mechanism, and even familiar operators raise unseen situations as optimization proceeds. We present ASCENDEVOLVE, which grounds every step of an evolutionary kernel search in a large external knowledge base built from platform documentation, toolkit headers, and operator repositories. Because this corpus far exceeds any context window, ASCENDEVOLVE indexes it and routes each agent to a small, task-specific slice of evidence instead of the corpus itself. The retrieved knowledge seeds diverse implementation strategies, guides repair when a candidate fails, and constrains how strong candidates are recombined. Then, we evaluate 6 frontier LLMs and find that all of them are vulnerable to show-up attacks. For example, GPT-5.6-Sol as a supervisor suffers an ASR of up to 0.93. | **[HABIT-Bench: Evaluating Habit Induction from Longitudinal Weak Evidence in Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24520v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In long-term interactions with large language model (LLM) agents, users reveal habits through their choices and actions. | **[More Memory, Worse Agents: Error Reproduction and Anti-Persistence in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27406v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents can improve across tasks without updating model parameters by storing experience from their own executions in external memory and reusing it to guide later decisions. However, errors in stored abstractions can propagate to subsequent decisions through retrieval and reuse. They can also shape execution trajectories, which then become the basis for new abstractions that reproduce the original errors. We term this feedback mechanism error reproduction: errors are regenerated through a cycle of abstraction, execution, and re-abstraction, extending their persistence beyond direct reuse of the original faulty knowledge. We propose Anti-Persistence, a memory design that accumulates trajectories and outcomes while reconstructing task-specific guidance on demand. An adaptive selector uses prior outcomes to decide whether and how to synthesize guidance, which is discarded after execution. This prevents its direct reuse as a stored object, although indirect feedback through retained trajectories remains possible. Across different benchmarks and backbones, Anti-Persistence achieves higher overall held-out success and the lowest average LLM-call cost among the evaluated memory designs. | **[ProMemAgent: Long-Horizon Proactive Assistance with Context-Aware Prospective Memory from Sensory Perceptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27400v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are moving beyond reactive responses toward proactive assistance that anticipates user needs without explicit requests. In this paper, we introduce ProMemAgent, the first proactive agent to incorporate prospective memory over everyday sensory contexts for long-horizon proactive assistance. ProMemAgent organizes past sensory perceptions (e.g., egocentric video and audio) into context-aware memory, generating prospective cues and linking earlier events with subsequent developments for future retrieval. Given the current context, it retrieves relevant past events and performs memory-augmented proactive reasoning to determine whether assistance is needed. We further introduce CPMBench, the first benchmark for evaluating long-horizon proactive assistance over sensory contexts, comprising 1,000 instances across eight everyday categories. | **[AgenticVulHunter: Bringing Local Agentic Vulnerability Hunting to Your Terminal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27383v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Finding vulnerabilities early, before they can affect the production system, is important for reducing the risk of any software attacks. | **[REM: What to Store, When to Recall, What to Forget in Experience Memory for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27377v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents now autonomously complete long-horizon tasks, such as repository-level coding, through hundreds of think-act steps, yet the experience each run produces is rarely reused on the next. We introduce REM (Reusable Experience Memory), a framework that manages the memory lifecycle from inside the agent's think-act loop through three explicit decisions. What to store: each completed trajectory is consolidated into a phase-structured document, two orders of magnitude smaller than the trajectory. When to recall: the document is injected at the start of the task; the costlier implementation record is withheld until an in-loop gate detects the agent struggling. What to forget: the record is evicted after a single exposure. On repository-level bug fixing (SWE-ContextBench Lite), REM retrieves the related trajectory more accurately than every state-of-the-art memory system (68.7% vs. 55.6% match@1) and improves, in every replicate, end-task resolution (27.6% vs. 24.9%) at 17% lower cost. | **[WhatIf: Interactive Exploration of Large-Scale LLM Agent Simulations for Policy Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17615v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Policymakers in domains such as emergency management, public health, and urban planning must make decisions under deep uncertainty, where outcomes depend on how large populations interpret information, coordinate, and adopt over time. Our findings show that participants used the system as a space for iterative branching and comparison rather than evaluating fixed plans; reflected on tacit planning assumptions when agent behavior violated expectations; surfaced previously unrecognized planning vulnerabilities; and grounded their reasoning in inspectable agent-level cases rather than aggregate outputs alone. These findings suggest broader design implications for LLM-powered social simulation systems: designing such systems as interactive, shared reasoning environments--rather than offline predictive tools--can better support expert decision-making under deep uncertainty.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ACM Symposium on User Interface Software and Technology (UIST)</p></details> |

Our evaluation shows that existing steering methods remain unreliable across realistic control objectives: they often fail to consistently induce the intended behavior and can substantially degrade the model's underlying task performance. Top-line EGI ranges from 39.6 (GPT-4o mini) to 65.6 (gpt-5.5), and no single model dominates every capability axis: gpt-5.5 leads task outcome, planning, and retrieval grounding; Claude Sonnet 4.5 leads tool actioning and safety/abstention; and Claude Haiku 4.5 leads engineering quality. When facts change and each memory is judged only by its own verified outcomes, one number, the certification load, sets how much reasoning any safe memory can skip, and under Zipf demand it yields a scaling law. During training, \method{} completes the task twice from each selected state, once with a short reasoning allowance and once with a long allowance, and compares their final quality and total processing to estimate when longer reasoning is valuable. During inference, a controller weighs this estimate against learned prices on remaining-budget pressure and chooses either short or long reasoning without running both alternatives. We evaluate PORA across application, memory, code, retail, and mathematics tasks, achieving strong results across all settings. Evaluations of three Ontology Alignment Evaluation Initiative (OAEI) tracks over state-of-the-art OM systems show that our system can achieve results very close to the long-standing best performance on simple OM tasks and can significantly improve the performance on complex and few-shot OM tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>35 pa...</summary><p>35 pages - VLDB 2025 (Page 1-20), OM 2025 (Page 21-31), OM 2026 (Page 32-35)</p></details> |
| **[Fake News, Real Crashes: Information Injection Attacks on LLM Financial Trading Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26438v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents now trade real capital using news as evidence, yet they cannot reliably tell fabricated headlines from real news. Because they read the same feeds under near-identical policies, a single fabricated post can push them into the same trade at the same moment. We present a systematic attack benchmark for information injection against LLM trading agents. Unlike prompt injection, which targets the model's instructions, information injection targets the evidence the agent trusts. The benchmark spans five injection vectors and seven event categories, evaluated over ~10,000 decisions on 15 U.S. stocks. Five findings emerge. (i) Prompt injection achieves 14% attack success rate (ASR), but fabricated news reaches 62% and two 30-word templates reach 100% on all 15 stocks, inverting the usual LLM-security intuition. (ii) For fact-poisoning templates, surface form matters more than content, since removing credibility cues such as a named source or a "BREAKING" tag drops ASR from 100% to 0% while the fake claim remains identical. (iii) Success depends on the agent's state, with a fake indicator panel scoring 91% against an agent about to hold but 12% against one about to buy. (iv) Attacks generalize across model vendors and remain effective when blended, unlabeled, into real news feeds. (v) Most attacks evade standard defenses, since model scaling stops shallow vectors but not fact poisoning, peer debate fails, and only hierarchical aggregation reduces ASR.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 7 figures, 18 tables</p></details> |

We argue that addressing these challenges requires more productive task-aligned exploration to improve the agent's understanding of environment dynamics. Motivated by intrinsic curiosity-driven exploration, we propose a conditional state-space exploration framework that balances task completion with intrinsic information-seeking behavior. Specifically, we introduce an intrinsic reward that penalizes actions whose observation duplicates prior state memory. Strong experimental results demonstrate that our approach substantially improves policy learning in multi-turn environments, mitigating invalid actions and repetitive loops.</p></details> |  |

We introduce cxbench, a benchmark that evaluates outcome and process compliance separately across six regulated domains. Each task combines a customer request, a tool-based environment, and explicit process requirements. Rule cards link cited regulatory obligations to observable trace evidence. | **[CyberArena: Benchmarking AI Agents in Live Symmetric Attack-and-Defense Combat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19005v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are demonstrating increasingly strong offensive and defensive cybersecurity capabilities in the real world. To address this gap, we present CyberArena, a dynamic, symmetric adversarial benchmark built around multi-round attack-and-defense combat. In isolated sandboxes, two agents compete under identical rules, acting concurrently as both attackers and defenders over continuously changing services. This converts static targets into evolving, semi-open challenges, directly testing an agent's speed and precision in discovering, exploiting, and patching vulnerabilities. To sustain the benchmark's longevity, an automated agentic pipeline continuously generates, reviews, and validates new environments. Furthermore, because pairwise contests are notoriously hard to quantify, we introduce a dynamical model that maps match dynamics into unified, multidimensional scores across offensive, defensive, and domain-specific capabilities. Evaluating 12 frontier LLM agents across 40 challenges in 466 matches, CyberArena separates model capabilities where single-agent baselines hit a ceiling. The trajectories read like a fight rather than an exam: defenders patch one hole and miss the next and certify repairs on stale evidence, while capable agents think a move ahead, planting backdoors for later rounds and, in one case, writing their own packet sniffer to watch the opponent. | **[Maestro: Agentic Formal Proof Development by Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25920v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lean is increasingly used to verify mathematical proofs and software produced by LLM agents, but writing formal proofs remains hard. Existing prover systems take a divide-and-conquer approach that recursively decomposes hard proof obligations into easier lemmas and splits again any lemma that resists proof. However, a lemma can resist proof for different reasons, including insufficient guidance, a false statement, or a flaw in the decomposition that produced it. Existing systems cannot tell these apart as a failed attempt reports little beyond its failure, and responses are confined to the failing node without considering the rest of the proof or revising the plan above it. The system may thus keep decomposing obligations along a flawed proof direction until the budget runs out, causing the whole proof attempt to fail. We introduce Maestro, a Lean prover harness that handles failures through global coordination. Provers report why they failed, not just that they failed, and a coordinator that sees the whole proof chooses an appropriate response, such as guiding a stuck prover, correcting a false lemma, or retracting a flawed subtree and replanning it. The same harness extends to verified code synthesis, where a checked counterexample sends the code back for repair.
